On June 8, 2023, Newsom proposed a 28th amendment to the U.S. Constitution to raise the age to buy firearms to 21, institute universal background checks for gun purchases, mandate waiting periods and ban assault weapons for civilians.[^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262] Law professor [Erwin Chemerinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erwin_Chemerinsky) called this a "terrible idea", since the advocated method (which has never been used) would be a [constitutional convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_to_propose_amendments_to_the_United_States_Constitution) (which is not understood to be limited to single amendments), potentially allowing a complete rewrite of the [Constitution](31644.md), or addition of other amendments on separate subjects, like abortion, or the often proposed [balanced budget amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balanced_budget_amendment) (which liberals feel would decimate welfare programs).[^cite_note-LAT_2023-06-08-263]

### Abortion

Further information: [Abortion in California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_California)

In December 2021, Newsom announced his intention to make California a "sanctuary" for abortion, which included possibly paying for procedures, travel, and lodging for out-of-state abortion seekers, if the procedure is banned in Republican-led states.[^cite_note-264] In March 2022, he signed a bill requiring private health insurance plans in the state to fully cover abortion procedures by eliminating associated co-pays and deductibles and increasing insurance premiums.[^cite_note-265] In February 2023, Newsom organized the [Reproductive Freedom Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproductive_Freedom_Alliance) of state governors supportive of abortion and reproductive rights.[^cite_note-266] After [Walgreens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walgreens) announced in March 2023 that it would refuse to dispense abortion pills in the 21 states where it is illegal, Newsom tweeted, "California won't be doing business with @walgreens – or any company that cowers to the extremists and puts women's lives at risk, we're done." He also said that Walgreens was giving in to "right-wing bullies" and that he would determine how California could cut ties with it.[^cite_note-267] He indicated that he wanted to cancel Walgreens's \$54 million contract with the California state prison system.[^cite_note-SFC_2023-04-06-268] Walgreens also receives \$1.5 billion for filling prescriptions for the 15 million people enrolled in the state's [Medi-Cal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medi-Cal) program (California's version of [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid)).[^cite_note-SFC_2023-04-06-268] Legal experts said that federal Medicaid laws do not allow health plans to disqualify providers for reasons other than fraud or contract violations, provisions that have prevented conservative states from blocking Medicaid spending to [Planned Parenthood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_Parenthood) clinics.[^cite_note-SFC_2023-04-06-268]

### Health

Further information: [Healthcare in California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_California)

Newsom campaigned on reducing the cost of health care and increasing access. He also indicated his support for creating a universal state health-care system.[^cite_note-SFC_2019-06-14-269] The budget passed in June 2019 expanded eligibility for [Medi-Cal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medi-Cal) from solely undocumented minor children to undocumented young adults from ages 19 to 25.[^cite_note-SFC_2019-06-14-269] In 2021, Newsom signed legislation expanding Medi-Cal eligibility to undocumented residents over age 50.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271] On June 30, 2022, he signed a \$307.9 billion state budget that "pledges to make all low-income adults eligible for the state's Medicaid program by 2024 regardless of their immigration status." This budget would make California the first U.S. state to guarantee healthcare to all low-income undocumented immigrants, at a cost of \$2.7 billion per year.[^cite_note-272]

Newsom was criticized in early 2022 for walking back from his support for [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care) and not supporting [CalCare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CalCare), Assembly Bill 1400, which would have instituted [single-payer healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-payer_healthcare) in California; critics suggested that opposition from business interests, which had donated large sums to Newsom and his party, had swayed his opinion.[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274]

On July 6, 2022, Newsom signed Senate Bill 184, which established the Office of Health Care Affordability, with the stated goal to "develop data-informed policies and enforceable cost targets, with the ultimate goal of containing health care costs".[^cite_note-275] In August 2022, Newsom vetoed Senate Bill 57 which sought to authorize jurisdictions to approve [supervised injection sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supervised_injection_sites).[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277] Newsom signed [The Zacky Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Zacky_Bill).[^cite_note-278] In October 2023, Newsom vetoed a bill to cap [co-pays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-pay) for [insulin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insulin_\(medication\)) at \$35.[^cite_note-279][^cite_note-280] In 2025, Newsom signed a budget that restricted new enrollment of undocumented immigrants in Medi-Cal.[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282] Newsom signed the [California Senate Bill 41 (2025)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Senate_Bill_41_\(2025\)) but vetoed other measures to regulate [pharmacy benefit managers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmacy_benefit_managers).[^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284][^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286]

#### Medical aid in dying

In 2021, Newsom signed Senate Bill 380, which amended the [California End of Life Option Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_End_of_Life_Option_Act) by shortening the required interval between a patient's two oral requests for an aid-in-dying drug from 15 days to 48 hours, eliminated the requirement for a final attestation, and extended the Act's expiration date to January 1, 2031.[^cite_note-287] In 2025, he signed Senate Bill 403, which repealed the provision setting that expiration date, thereby extending the Act indefinitely.[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-\:12-7]

### Infrastructure and development

#### High-speed rail

For broader coverage of this topic, see [California High-Speed Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_High-Speed_Rail).

In his February 2019 State of the State address, Newsom announced that, while work would continue on the 171-mile (275 km)[^cite_note-289] Central Valley segment from Bakersfield to Merced, the rest of the system would be indefinitely postponed, citing cost overruns and delays.[^cite_note-290] This and other actions created tension with the State Building and Construction Trades Council of California, a [labor union](2474406.md) representing 450,000 members.[^cite_note-291]

#### Homelessness and housing shortage

For broader coverage of this topic, see [Homelessness in California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homelessness_in_California) and [California housing shortage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_housing_shortage).

A poll found that California voters thought the most important issue for Newsom and the state legislature to work on in 2020 was homelessness.[^cite_note-292] In his first week of office, Newsom threatened to withhold state funding for infrastructure to communities that failed to take actions to alleviate California's housing shortage.[^cite_note-USA_100_days-293][^cite_note-294] In late January 2019, he announced that he would sue Huntington Beach for preventing the construction of affordable housing.[^cite_note-295] A year later, the city acted to settle the lawsuit by the state.[^cite_note-296] Newsom opposes [NIMBY](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NIMBY) (not-in-my-back-yard) sentiment, declaring in 2022 that "NIMBYism is destroying the state".[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299][^cite_note-NIMBYismDestroyingState-300] In 2021, he signed a pair of bills into law that made zoning regulations for housing less restrictive, allowing construction of duplexes and fourplexes in lots that were previously zoned exclusively for single-family homes.[^cite_note-301] Newsom also signed a bill that expedites the environmental review process for new multifamily developments worth at least \$15,000,000. To participate, developers must apply directly through the governor's office.[^cite_note-302]

In 2022, Newsom signed 39 bills into law intended to address California's housing crisis, three of which entailed major land use reform.[^cite_note-\:5-303] One bill eliminated minimum parking requirements for housing near mass transit stations throughout the state.[^cite_note-khouri-304] Michael Manville, an urban planning professor at UCLA's Luskin School of Public Affairs, called it "one of the biggest land-use reforms in the country". Another bill allowed developers to build housing on some lots previously zoned exclusively for commercial use without getting local governments' permission if a certain percentage of the housing was affordable.[^cite_note-\:6-305] A third bill allowed for the construction of market-rate housing on some lots previously zoned exclusively for commercial use.[^cite_note-\:6-305] At the signing ceremony for the latter two bills, Newsom warned local governments, which have a history of blocking and delaying housing developments, that they would be held accountable for future housing obstructionism.[^cite_note-\:5-303] Other bills he signed required localities "to approve or deny various building permits within a strict timeline" and streamlined student and faculty housing projects by allowing California colleges to skip onerous review processes for new projects.[^cite_note-\:5-303] Measures and actions to reduce homelessness in California have not yet solved the problem.[^cite_note-watt-306] The number of homeless reached a record 181,000 in January 2023.[^cite_note-levin-307] According to some, to cope with this problem, California must build more than 2.5 million housing units.[^cite_note-watt-306]



Newsom meeting with President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) and First Lady [Melania Trump](1209075.md)

In October 2023, Newsom vetoed several bills aimed at expanding access to housing assistance including the [California Social Housing Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Social_Housing_Act). One was a bill to repurpose unused state-owned land for affordable housing, which he said infringed on state sovereignty. Another would have expanded the number of people who qualify for state housing assistance. A third would have mandated that [Medi-Cal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medi-Cal) cover the cost of housing assistance.[^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310] Newsom has signed the [Abundant and Affordable Homes Near Transit Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abundant_and_Affordable_Homes_Near_Transit_Act), [Affordable Housing on Faith and Higher Education Lands Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Housing_on_Faith_and_Higher_Education_Lands_Act), [California Assembly Bill 2097 (2022)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Assembly_Bill_2097_\(2022\)) and [California HOME Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_HOME_Act). In August 2024, Newsom warned counties that did not remove their homeless encampments that failure to do so would result in their state funding being cut off the next year.[^cite_note-311] He issued this warning after personally visiting and clearing out a Los Angeles homeless encampment without notifying the city beforehand.[^cite_note-312] In September 2024, Newsom vetoed Assembly Bill 1840, which sought to prohibit the disqualification of applicants to [California Housing Finance Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Housing_Finance_Agency) home purchase assistance programs based solely on their immigration status.[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314]

#### Water management

Further information: [California Water Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Water_Plan)

Newsom supports a series of tentative water-sharing agreements that would bring an end to the dispute between farmers, cities, fishers, and environmentalists over how much water should be left in the state's two most important rivers, the [Sacramento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramento_River) and [San Joaquin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Joaquin_River), which flow into the [Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramento%E2%80%93San_Joaquin_River_Delta).[^cite_note-315]

### LGBTQ+ rights

For broader coverage of this topic, see [LGBT rights in California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_California).

In September 2022, Newsom signed [Senate Bill 107](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Senate_Bill_107) into law,[^cite_note-316] making California the first sanctuary state for transgender youth. He issued an official state proclamation for [Pride Month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pride_Month) in June 2023,[^cite_note-317] issued a fine of \$1.5 million to a [school district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temecula_Valley_Unified_School_District) whose board rejected a curriculum including a biography of gay rights leader [Harvey Milk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvey_Milk),[^cite_note-318] and signed a bill prohibiting schools from banning textbooks based on their inclusion of references to people from minority groups or the LGBT community.[^cite_note-319] In July 2024, he signed the "[SAFETY Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safety_Act_\(California_law\))", which prohibits schools from [outing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outing) students' [gender identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_identity) to their parents without the students' consent.[^cite_note-320]

Newsom later vetoed several bills that the Assembly had passed by a wide margin, one of which would have instructed judges who preside over custody battles to take into account parents' affirmation of their children's gender identity, and one of which would have mandated that insurance plans serving California residents cover the cost of gender-affirming care.[^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322] This, and later comments by Newsom on his podcast calling [trans women's participation in women's sports](46676322.md) "deeply unfair", appeared to signify a change in course in which he forsook his support of key transgender issues to appeal to right-wing audiences. He received backlash from some progressive commentators[^cite_note-\:13-323][^cite_note-\:14-324] and the disapproval and condemnation of several of his fellow Democrats, such as California State Assemblyman [Chris Ward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Ward_\(California_politician\)) and California State Senator [Caroline Menjivar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caroline_Menjivar) of the LGBTQ+ Caucus, who said his comments "profoundly sickened and frustrated" them.[^cite_note-\:14-324] Newsom has also been condemned and criticized by humanitarian and queer rights organizations such as the [Human Rights Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign), [National Center for LGBTQ Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Center_for_LGBTQ_Rights), and the [Los Angeles LGBT Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_LGBT_Center). The [American Civil Liberties Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_Liberties_Union) issued a statement saying it was "saddened and angered" by Newsom's conduct, calling on him to "lean into his role as a leader on LGBTQ+ issues and reject the hateful, extremist rhetoric".[^cite_note-\:14-324][^cite_note-\:15-325][^cite_note-\:16-326][^cite_note-\:17-327]

In 2026, Newsom vetoed a bill that would have protected transgender healthcare and abortion providers from being extradited to other states. In his veto message, he said he supported protecting these services and their providers but expressed concern about the executive function of extradition.[^cite_note-328]

### Labor rights

Newsom signed [California Assembly Bill 5 (2019)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Assembly_Bill_5_\(2019\)).[^cite_note-329] He also signed the [California FAST Recovery Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_FAST_Recovery_Act) (2022).[^cite_note-330] In October 2023, Newsom vetoed a bill to provide unemployment insurance to striking workers, citing excess burden on the state's unemployment system.[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332] He also vetoed a bill to expand the mandatory warning given to employees soon to be laid off from 60 days to 75, extend the same protections to long-term contract workers, and prohibit employers from making laid-off employees sign nondisclosure agreements in order to receive severance.[^cite_note-333] In 2024, Newsom vetoed SB 1299, which "would have required workers' comp judges to presume farmworkers who claim heat illness developed it at work".[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336] Newsom vetoed legislation seeking to extend [unemployment benefits](378430.md) to undocumented workers.[^cite_note-337]

### Elections

In October 2023, Newsom vetoed Assembly Bill 1248, which sought to require independent redistricting.[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339] In September 2024, Newsom vetoed Assembly Bill 2724, which sought to require schools to ensure students receive information on how to pre-register to vote.[^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341]

#### 2025 California Proposition 50

Main article: [2025 California Proposition 50](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_California_Proposition_50)

In August 2025, Newsom secured legislative approval of California Proposition 50, a state constitutional amendment to allow congressional [redistricting](667093.md). The goal was to switch seats from Republican to Democratic control in response to the [2025 Texas gerrymander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Texas_gerrymander) that would add five new Republican seats.[^cite_note-342][^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344] Voters approved the measure in November.[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346]

Newsom has been widely considered a prominent figure in [liberal American politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism_in_the_United_States) during the 2020s.[^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350] In 2023, he launched Campaign for Democracy, a [political action committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_action_committee) to take on "authoritarian leaders" in the U.S. It is thought to be a starting point for a possible 2028 presidential bid.[^cite_note-351] By July 2024, Campaign for Democracy had raised \$24 million for direct contributions to candidates and other spending.[^cite_note-352] The group is not subject to contribution limits and can coordinate with Newsom as long as he is not a candidate for federal office.[^cite_note-353]

Many journalists and political analysts have mentioned Newsom as a presidential hopeful. According to a June 2023 poll by [NewsNation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NewsNation), 22% of California voters wanted him to enter the [2024 U.S. presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_U.S._presidential_election).[^cite_note-354] In May 2023, [Arnold Schwarzenegger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnold_Schwarzenegger) said it was a "no-brainer" that Newsom would someday run for president.[^cite_note-355] An April 2023 *[The Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hill_\(newspaper\))* article by Sharon Udasin also discussed the inevitability of a Newsom presidential run.[^cite_note-356] In September 2022, Newsom said he would not run for president in 2024, citing his "vulnerable" 2021 recall.[^cite_note-357] After his 2022 reelection, he told White House staff that he would not challenge President [Joe Biden](145422.md) in the [2024 Democratic Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries);[^cite_note-358] he endorsed [Biden's reelection campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_2024_presidential_campaign) on April 25, 2023.[^cite_note-359]

Newsom has become an outspoken critic of the policies of Florida governor [Ron DeSantis](36729527.md), denouncing DeSantis's orchestration of the [Martha's Vineyard migrant airlift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martha's_Vineyard_migrant_airlift).[^cite_note-360] DeSantis responded by saying California has "huge problems" and dared Newsom to run against Biden.[^cite_note-361] In November 2023, the two debated, with [Fox News](11121.md)'s [Sean Hannity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sean_Hannity) as moderator.[^cite_note-362] In July 2024, Newsom launched a podcast, *Politickin'*, co-hosted by [Marshawn Lynch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshawn_Lynch) and Doug Hendrickson.[^cite_note-363] After Biden's sudden [withdrawal from the presidential race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Joe_Biden_from_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election) that month, Newsom said he would not seek the Democratic nomination, and endorsed Vice President [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) for president.[^cite_note-364]

After [Donald Trump](4848272.md) won the 2024 election, Newsom called for California lawmakers to convene later in 2024 to safeguard California's policies from the [second Trump administration](78298108.md).[^cite_note-365] In December 2024, Newsom criticized Biden for pardoning his son [Hunter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_Biden),[^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367] saying, "I'm disappointed and can't support the decision."[^cite_note-368] Since the [2024 U.S. presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_U.S._presidential_election), Newsom has repeatedly declined to identify himself with either the [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism_in_the_United_States) or [moderate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moderate_Democrat) [faction of the Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Factions_in_the_Democratic_Party_\(United_States\)), calling such labels "reductive" and calling himself a "hard-headed pragmatist". He said he wants the Democratic Party to be an "inclusive" [big tent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_tent), spanning the ideological spectrum "from [Manchin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Manchin) to [Mamdani](64618347.md)".[^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370] In August 2025, Newsom began communicating on social media platforms in a style intended to mock Trump's style of online communication.[^cite_note-371]

In June 2026, Newsom said the [United States Department of Justice](52563.md) was prosecuting him and his wife at Trump's direction.[^cite_note-372]

### 2028 presidential election

Newsom has expressed interest in running for [president](24113.md) of the United States in [2028](77541080.md).[^cite_note-\:9-2]

In anticipation of a potential [2028 U.S. presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2028_U.S._presidential_election) campaign, Newsom has adjusted his political approach to appeal to a broader electorate. A key shift in his strategy has been his engagement with conservative voices, including hosting Trump supporters such as [Charlie Kirk](53245776.md) and [Steve Bannon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Bannon) on his podcast, *This is Gavin Newsom*.[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-\:13-323] This tactic has been criticized by some humanitarian and [queer rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queer_rights) organizations both inside and outside California, including state legislators, [Human Rights Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign), and the [American Civil Liberties Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_Liberties_Union).[^cite_note-\:14-324][^cite_note-\:15-325][^cite_note-\:16-326][^cite_note-\:17-327] Many commentators viewed his widely publicized August 2025 [Election Rigging Response Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Rigging_Response_Act) as a soft launch of a 2028 presidential run.[^cite_note-374] In May 2026, [Reed Hastings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reed_Hastings) was the first Democrat mega-donor to publicly declare his support for a potential Newsom presidential campaign.[^cite_note-375]

## Personal life



Newsom with his then-fiancée [Jennifer Siebel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jennifer_Siebel_Newsom) at the 2008 [San Francisco Pride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Pride) parade

Newsom was [baptized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptized) and raised in his father's [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic) faith. In 2008, he described himself as an "[Irish Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Catholic) rebel ... in some respects, but one that still has tremendous admiration for the Church and very strong faith"; when asked about the state of the Catholic Church, Newsom said it was in crisis.[^cite_note-Santa-19] He said he stays with the Church because of his "strong connection to a greater purpose, and to sort of a higher being".[^cite_note-Santa-19] Newsom identifies as a practicing Catholic,[^cite_note-376] saying in 2008 that he has a "strong sense of faith that is perennial, day in and day out".[^cite_note-Santa-19] He has personal ties to the Jewish community. As a child, Newsom attended Hanukkah celebrations with Jewish extended family, went to a [JCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Community_Center) preschool in San Francisco, and spent time at the Jewish summer camp [Camp Tawonga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Tawonga). He credits the Jewish values he absorbed—especially [tikkun olam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tikkun_olam)—for shaping his identity and worldview and helping him succeed in business and politics. Newsom told his son that they are partly Jewish and have Jewish cousins in the Newsom family when one of his friends expressed antisemitic views. In 2019, he took steps to combat antisemitism by allocating \$15 million for synagogue security, \$6 million for the [Holocaust Museum LA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_Museum_LA), and \$23.5 million for Jewish summer camps affected by wildfires.[^cite_note-\:8-377]

In May 2002, Newsom was at his mother's side as she died by [physician-assisted suicide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physician-assisted_suicide). She had been suffering from breast cancer and made the choice to die. Assisted suicide was not legal in California at that time.[^cite_note-\:12-7][^cite_note-378]

After completing his service as mayor of San Francisco in 2011, Newsom and his family moved to a house they bought in [Kentfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kentfield,_California) in [Marin County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marin_County,_California) in 2012.[^cite_note-379] After his election as governor, Newsom and his family moved into the [California Governor's Mansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor's_Mansion_State_Historic_Park) in [Downtown Sacramento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Sacramento) and thereafter settled in [Fair Oaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Oaks,_California).[^cite_note-Fair_Oaks-380] In May 2019, *[The Sacramento Bee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sacramento_Bee)* reported that Newsom's \$3.7 million purchase of a 12,000-square-foot home (1,100 m<sup>2</sup>) in Fair Oaks was the most expensive private residence sold in the Sacramento region since the year began.[^cite_note-Fair_Oaks-380] In August 2021, Newsom sold the Marin County home for \$5.9 million in an off-market transaction. He had originally put the property up for sale in early 2019 for \$5.895 million, but removed the property from the market after a price reduction to \$5.695 million.[^cite_note-381]

In February 2026, Newsom released *Young Man in a Hurry: A Memoir of Discovery*, a book about his family and early life.[^cite_note-382]

### Relationships and family

In December 2001, Newsom married legal commentator [Kimberly Guilfoyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimberly_Guilfoyle) at [Saint Ignatius Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Ignatius_Church_\(San_Francisco\)).[^cite_note-Reflects-17][^cite_note-383] They separated in 2004 and jointly filed for divorce in January 2005, citing "difficulties due to their careers on opposite coasts".[^cite_note-Coasts-384] Their divorce was finalized on February 28, 2006, by which time Guilfoyle was expecting a child with [Eric Villency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Villency).[^cite_note-385] Guilfoyle gained prominence in 2011 via a [Fox News](11121.md) chat show.[^cite_note-386] She was later named senior advisor to Republican president [Donald Trump](4848272.md), whom Newsom has extensively criticized, and was later engaged to [Donald Trump Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_Jr.)[^cite_note-387]

In September 2006, Newsom, then 38, briefly dated 19-year-old Brittanie Mountz, a model and restaurant hostess.[^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389] On January 31, 2007, Newsom's close friend, campaign manager, and former [chief of staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_staff) Alex Tourk confronted him after learning from his wife, Ruby Rippey-Tourk, that she and Newsom had an affair in 2005, when she was Newsom's appointments secretary. Tourk immediately resigned.[^cite_note-390] Newsom admitted to the affair the next day and apologized to the public, saying he was "deeply sorry" for his "personal lapse of judgment".[^cite_note-391] In 2018, Rippey-Tourk said that she thought it wrong to associate Newsom's behavior with the [#MeToo](55551931.md) movement: "I was a subordinate, but I was also a free-thinking, 33-yr old adult married woman and mother. I do want to make sure that the #metoo movement is reserved for cases and situations that deserve it."[^cite_note-392]

Newsom began dating film director [Jennifer Siebel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jennifer_Siebel) in October 2006.[^cite_note-393] He announced he would seek treatment for [alcohol use disorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_use_disorder) in February 2007.[^cite_note-394] The couple announced their engagement in December 2007,[^cite_note-NewsomEngaged-395][^cite_note-NewsomWedding-396] and they were married in [Stevensville, Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stevensville,_Montana), in July 2008.[^cite_note-Park-397] They have four children: a daughter born in 2009, a son born in 2011, a daughter born in 2013, and a son born in 2016.[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399][^cite_note-400][^cite_note-401]

Newsom is the godfather of designer, model, and [LGBTQ](78174325.md) rights activist [Nats Getty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nats_Getty).[^cite_note-402][^cite_note-403] According to James Reginato's *Growing Up Getty*, the Newsoms, [Harrises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harris_family), [Pelosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Pelosi), and Gettys are godparents to one another's children and make appearances at important family events.[^cite_note-404]

[^cite_note-LAT_2023-06-08-263]: &#x20;Willon, Phil; Luna, Taryn (June 8, 2023). ["Newsom launches long-shot push for U.S. constitutional amendment on gun control"](https://www.latimes.com/california/story/2023-06-08/newsom-calls-for-constitutional-amendment-on-gun-control). *[Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times)*. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230608153200/https://www.latimes.com/california/story/2023-06-08/newsom-calls-for-constitutional-amendment-on-gun-control) from the original on June 8, 2023. Under Article V of the U.S. Constitution, an amendment may be proposed by a two-thirds majority vote in both chambers of Congress or by a constitutional convention called for by two-thirds of the state legislatures. None of the 27 amendments to the Constitution have been proposed in a constitutional convention under that process. "To me, what's really frightening about it is we've never had a constitutional convention under that procedure of Article V," Chemerinsky said. "No one knows would it be limited to just the 2nd Amendment, or could it do anything? Could they do abortion or rewrite the Constitution? How is it going to be constituted? What are its rules?"

The federal regulations that protect Walgreens are the same that have allowed Planned Parenthood to offer health care services to Medicaid enrollees in conservative states, where leaders have sought unsuccessfully to exclude the network of clinics from receiving taxpayer funding. An approved pharmacy company can be excluded from state networks only if it has committed fraud or other contract violations, added Edwin Park, a research professor at Georgetown University with expertise in Medicaid law. "Certainly, that wouldn't be the case for Walgreens," Park said.
